=== PROMPT VARIANT ===
r1

=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given two positive integers n and k.
An integer x is called k-palindromic if:

x is a palindrome.
x is divisible by k.

An integer is called good if its digits can be rearranged to form a k-palindromic integer. For example, for k = 2, 2020 can be rearranged to form the k-palindromic integer 2002, whereas 1010 cannot be rearranged to form a k-palindromic integer.
Return the count of good integers containing n digits.
Note that any integer must not have leading zeros, neither before nor after rearrangement. For example, 1010 cannot be rearranged to form 101.
 
Example 1:

Input: n = 3, k = 5
Output: 27
Explanation:
Some of the good integers are:

551 because it can be rearranged to form 515.
525 because it is already k-palindromic.


Example 2:

Input: n = 1, k = 4
Output: 2
Explanation:
The two good integers are 4 and 8.

Example 3:

Input: n = 5, k = 6
Output: 2468

 
Constraints:

1 <= n <= 10
1 <= k <= 9

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python
class Solution:
    def countGoodIntegers(self, n: int, k: int) -> int:
        
```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

    bad //= factorial[cnt[0]-1]

return total

Let's write code:

from itertools import product
from math import factorial

class Solution:
    def countGoodIntegers(self, n: int, k: int) -> int:
        fact = [1]*(n+1)
        for i in range(1,n+1):
            fact[i] = fact[i-1]*i
        
        good = set()
        L = (n + 1) // 2
        
        for first in range(1, 10):
            for rest in product(range(10), repeat=L-1):
                seq = (first,) + rest
                s = ''.join(str(d) for d in seq)
                if n % 2 == 0:
                    pal = s + s[::-1]
                else:
                    pal = s + s[:-1][::-1]
                if int(pal) % k == 0:
                    cnt = [0]*10
                    for ch in pal:
                        cnt[ord(ch)-48] += 1
                    good.add(tuple(cnt))
        
        def count_numbers(cnt):
            total = fact[n]
            for c in cnt:
                total //= fact[c]
            if cnt[0] > 0:
                bad = fact[n-1]
                bad //= fact[cnt[0]-1]
                for d in range(1,10):
                    bad //= fact[cnt[d]]
                total -= bad
            return total
        
        ans = 0
        for cnt in good:
            ans += count_numbers(cnt)
        return ans

Need check fact[n-1] when n=1: fact[0]=1. cnt[0]>0 and cnt[0]=1, bad = fact[0] / fact[0] / prod =1. total=1, ans0. Good.

Potential issue: For n=9, L=5, same 90k. Actually n=9 palindromes determined by first5 digits, total 90k. Good.

Let digits set maybe includes a,b,c. a is even nonzero. b,c any. The multiset counts:
- If a,b,c all distinct: counts (2,2,1). There are two digits with count2: a and b; one count1: c.
- If a=b: counts (4,1) for digit a count4, c count1.
- If a=c: counts (3,2) for digit a count3, b count2.
- If b=c: counts (2,3) for digit a count2, b count3.
- If a=b=c: counts (5) for digit a count5.

Need count unique multisets satisfying existence of a,b,c with a even nonzero, c ≡ a+b mod3, and the count pattern as above. But note if a,b,c not distinct, the same multiset may be representable in multiple ways; we should count once. We can count by multiset pattern.

- (5): N=1 if digit nonzero? If digit 0 no because first digit nonzero and palindrome first nonzero; but a even nonzero so digit nonzero. Count1.

Let's think about code style. The final answer should be Python code in a code block. We can include imports at top. The starter code has class Solution. We'll output:

```python
from itertools import product

class Solution:
    def countGoodIntegers(self, n: int, k: int) -> int:
        fact = [1] * (n + 1)
        for i in range(1, n + 1):
            fact[i] = fact[i - 1] * i

        good = set()
        half_len = (n + 1) // 2

        for first in range(1, 10):
            for tail in product(range(10), repeat=half_len - 1):
                seq = (first,) + tail
                s = ''.join(str(d) for d in seq)
                if n % 2 == 0:
                    pal = s + s[::-1]
                else:
                    pal = s + s[:-1][::-1]

                if int(pal) % k == 0:
                    cnt = [0] * 10
                    for ch in pal:
                        cnt[ord(ch) - 48] += 1
                    good.add(tuple(cnt))

        def count_numbers(cnt):
            total = fact[n]
            for c in cnt:
                total //= fact[c]

            if cnt[0] > 0:
                bad = fact[n - 1]
                bad //= fact[cnt[0] - 1]
                for d in range(1, 10):
                    bad //= fact[cnt[d]]
                total -= bad

            return total

        return sum(count_numbers(cnt) for cnt in good)
```

=== ANSWER ===
>
```python
from itertools import product

class Solution:
    def countGoodIntegers(self, n: int, k: int) -> int:
        fact = [1] * (n + 1)
        for i in range(1, n + 1):
            fact[i] = fact[i - 1] * i

        good = set()
        half_len = (n + 1) // 2

        for first in range(1, 10):
            for tail in product(range(10), repeat=half_len - 1):
                seq = (first,) + tail
                s = ''.join(str(d) for d in seq)

                if n % 2 == 0:
                    pal = s + s[::-1]
                else:
                    pal = s + s[:-1][::-1]

                if int(pal) % k == 0:
                    cnt = [0] * 10
                    for ch in pal:
                        cnt[ord(ch) - 48] += 1
                    good.add(tuple(cnt))

        def count_numbers(cnt):
            total = fact[n]
            for c in cnt:
                total //= fact[c]

            if cnt[0] > 0:
                bad = fact[n - 1]
                bad //= fact[cnt[0] - 1]
                for d in range(1, 10):
                    bad //= fact[cnt[d]]
                total -= bad

            return total

        return sum(count_numbers(cnt) for cnt in good)
```
